Today, it is one of the most preventable cancers through human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccination and routine cervical cancer screening. The HPV vaccine also protects boys and girls against other HPV-related cancers that can develop later in life. Yet thousands of women and men in the United States are still diagnosed with HPV-related cancers each year, and thousands more lose their lives to diseases that, in many cases, can be prevented or detected early. That reality represents both a challenge and an opportunity. The Role of HPV Vaccination Nearly all cervical cancers are caused by persistent infection with certain types of HPV, a common virus that most people will encounter at some point in their lives. The HPV vaccine - known by its brand name Gardasil - protects against aggressive HPV types responsible for most cervical cancers and several other cancers as well. HPV vaccination provides safe, effective, and long-lasting protection for both boys and girls. HPV can cause cancers in women and men, including cervical, throat, anal and penile cancers. HPV is responsible for an estimated 60-70% of oropharyngeal cancers in the United States, and these cancers occur far more often in men than women. Vaccinating all children at the recommended age helps protect them from HPV-related cancers later in life. With more than 135 million doses distributed in the United States, the HPV vaccine has a reassuring safety record that's backed by over 15 years of monitoring and research. And the data show it works: HPV infections, genital warts, and cervical pre-cancers (abnormal cells on the cervix that can lead to cancer) have dropped since the vaccine was introduced in the United States. Infections with HPV types that cause most HPV cancers and genital warts have dropped 88% among teen girls and 81% among young adult women. And among vaccinated women, the percentage of cervical pre-cancers caused by the HPV types most often linked to cervical cancer has dropped by 40%. Since the introduction of Gardasil, healthcare professionals have viewed HPV vaccination as one of the most promising cancer prevention tools available. Today, we are beginning to see the impact of that investment. Research published this year by the American Cancer Society found that cervical cancer incidence among women ages 20 to 31 declined 27% nationally in the years following HPV vaccine introduction, with states that achieved higher vaccination rates generally experiencing larger declines. Higher vaccination coverage was associated with greater reductions in cervical cancer risk. Even more striking, new observational research published in The Lancet found that in England, where HPV vaccination rates have been high, no cervical cancer deaths occurred among women ages 20 to 24 between 2020 and 2024, compared with more than 23 expected deaths based on historical trends. Researchers concluded that high HPV vaccination coverage was associated with substantial reductions in cervical cancer mortality. "The HPV vaccine is recommended beginning in adolescence because it is most effective before exposure to the virus," said Dr. Gourdine, SVP and Chief Medical Officer for CVS Caremark®, a trained pediatrician and public health leader. "When your child is 11 or 12 years old, it can feel uncomfortable to think about care connected to their future sexual activity-but this is one of the best tools we have to prevent cervical cancer, as well as other HPV-related cancers that can affect both women and men." Screening Remains Essential Vaccination is only one part of the prevention story. Routine cervical cancer screening remains one of the most effective ways to identify precancerous changes before they become cancer or to detect cancer at an earlier, more treatable stage. The Pap test and HPV test can identify abnormalities long before symptoms appear, creating opportunities for timely intervention and better outcomes. However, screening rates remain a concern. Data from the Office of Disease Prevention and Health Promotion show that cervical cancer screening rates have been declining for years and have not fully recovered to pre-pandemic levels. This trend is especially concerning because regular screening continues to be a cornerstone of cervical cancer prevention. "Cervical cancer screening remains essential, even for people who have received the HPV vaccine," said Dr. Joanne Armstrong, VP and Chief Medical Officer, Women's Health and Genomics, CVS Health®. "Following recommended screening guidelines can help identify precancerous changes before they become cancer, when treatment may be most effective." Prevention Works Best When We Use Every Tool Available Too often, conversations about cervical cancer focus only after a diagnosis. Yet some of the most important actions happen years earlier through preventive care. Vaccination helps reduce the risk of HPV infection. Screening helps detect abnormalities early. Together, these tools create a powerful opportunity to reduce the burden of cervical cancer and improve long-term health outcomes. As physicians, we see firsthand the value of prevention. We also recognize that navigating healthcare decisions can sometimes feel overwhelming. But cervical cancer prevention offers a rare example of a public health success story already within our reach. The science is clear: preventive care saves lives. By staying up to date on recommended vaccinations and routine screenings, patients can take meaningful steps to protect their health and reduce their risk of cervical cancer. While no prevention strategy is perfect, cervical cancer demonstrates what is possible when medical innovation, preventive care, and patient action come together. It is one of the clearest examples in modern medicine that the best cancer treatment may be preventing cancer from developing in the first place. Find more stories and multimedia from CVS Health at 3blmedia.com. 浦那实验室推出 HPV 自采样套件,使 宫颈癌 检测更容易进行 - ETV Bharat

Pune-based Lab Launches HPV Self-Sampling Kit To Make Cervical Cancer Testing More Accessible - ETV Bharat
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 6 日,总部位于浦那的 Greenarray Labs 推出了 CerviSens,这是一种用于 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 检测的自采样试剂盒,旨在使 宫颈癌 筛查变得更容易。该套件允许女性私下收集自己的阴道样本,从而无需进行窥器检查。该举措旨在解决尴尬、隐私问题和使用医疗设施的机会有限等障碍。自采样方法已经过临床验证,HPV检测的敏感性为98.0%,特异性为99.4%。对来自阿鲁纳恰尔邦马哈拉施特拉邦、卡纳塔克邦、奥里萨邦和伊塔那加尔的 12,373 个样本进行的多中心研究发现,2.6% 的样本 高危型HPV 呈阳性,其中 63% 的病例为 31-50 岁的女性。此外,34.5%的HPV阳性样本显示两种或多种高危基因型同时感染,包括HPV16与HPV66、HPV33与HPV58以及HPV18与HPV33等组合。该实验室强调将更简单的样本采集与经过验证的实验室测试相结合的重要性,并确保对测试呈阳性的人进行适当的医疗随访。世界卫生组织的 宫颈癌 消除策略包括 HPV 疫苗接种、筛查和及时 治疗,强调了此类举措在提高筛查参与度和识别高危感染方面的重要性。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pune-based Lab Launches HPV Self-Sampling Kit To Make Cervical Cancer Testing More Accessible - ETV Bharat

News Brief
On October 6, 2026, Pune-based Greenarray Labs launched CerviSens, a self-sampling kit for Human Papillomavirus (HPV) testing, designed to make cervical cancer screening more accessible. The kit allows women to collect their own vaginal samples in privacy, eliminating the need for a speculum examination. This initiative aims to address barriers such as embarrassment, privacy concerns, and limited access to healthcare facilities. The self-sampling method has been clinically validated, showing 98.0% sensitivity and 99.4% specificity for HPV detection. A multicentric study of 12,373 samples from Maharashtra, Karnataka, Odisha, and Itanagar in Arunachal Pradesh found that 2.6% of samples were positive for high-risk HPV, with 63% of these cases among women aged 31-50 years. Additionally, 34.5% of HPV-positive samples showed co-infection with two or more high-risk genotypes, including combinations like HPV16 with HPV66, HPV33 with HPV58, and HPV18 with HPV33. The lab emphasizes the importance of combining easier sample collection with validated laboratory testing and ensuring appropriate medical follow-up for those testing positive. The World Health Organization’s strategy for cervical cancer elimination includes HPV vaccination, screening, and timely treatment, highlighting the significance of such initiatives in increasing screening participation and identifying high-risk infections.
Original source text (English)
cec gyanesh kumarelection commissionflydubai incidentrahul gandhi ❯ E हिं অ বাং ગુ ಕ മ म ଓ ਪੰ த తె ار ❯ National English हिंदी उत्तर प्रदेशउत्तराखंडछत्तीसगढ़झारखंडदिल्लीबिहारमध्य प्रदेशराजस्थानहरियाणाहिमाचल प्रदेश অসমীয়া বাংলা ગુજરાતી ಕನ್ನಡ മലയാളം मराठी ଓଡିଆ ਪੰਜਾਬੀ தமிழ் తెలుగు ఆంధ్రప్రదేశ్తెలంగాణ اردو Live NATIONAL WORLD SPORTS ENTERTAINMENT OPINION PHOTOS HEALTH STATE BUSINESS More Live ETV Bharat / health Pune-based Lab Launches HPV Self-Sampling Kit To Make Cervical Cancer Testing More Accessible For some women, awkward, embarrassment, privacy concerns or limited access to healthcare facilities can become barriers to undergoing HPV testing. HPV16 was the most frequently detected genotype of the Human Papillomavirus in the study published in Frontiers in Medical Technology (Getty Images) By ETV Bharat Health Team Published : October 6, 2026 at 12:05 PM IST 4 Min Read In an effort to make cervical cancer screening more accessible to women, Pune-based Greenarray Labs has launched CerviSens, a vaginal self-sampling kit that allows women to collect their own sample for Human Papillomavirus (HPV) testing, without requiring a speculum examination at the time of sample collection. Cervical cancer is largely preventable through HPV vaccination, regular screening and timely treatment. However, screening continues to remain a challenge for many women. Conventional sample collection generally requires a visit to a healthcare facility, a trained healthcare professional and a pelvic examination using a speculum. Why Self-Sampling? For some women, awkward, embarrassment, privacy concerns or limited access to healthcare facilities can become barriers to undergoing screening. CerviSens has been developed to simplify the sample collection process by allowing women to collect a vaginal sample themselves in privacy. The collected sample is subsequently tested in a laboratory for high-risk HPV, which is associated with the majority of cervical cancer cases. The self-sampling approach has also undergone clinical validation. A study published in the international journal Frontiers in Medical Technology in September 2025 evaluated 203 paired self-collected and clinician-collected samples. The study reported very good agreement between the two collection methods. Using clinician-collected samples as the reference standard, CerviSens self-collected samples showed 98.0% sensitivity and 99.4% specificity for HPV detection. Dr. Sanjay Gupte, Founder, Greenarray Labs, said, “Cervical cancer is one of the cancers where screening can make a significant difference, yet many women still do not undergo regular screening. One of the reasons is that the conventional process of sample collection may be uncomfortable or difficult to access for some women. Self-sampling provides another way for women to participate in screening while maintaining their privacy and comfort. The important next step is ensuring that women who test positive are appropriately counselled and connected to further clinical evaluation and care.” Beyond making sample collection simpler, Greenarray Labs has also been studying the different high-risk HPV genotypes found among women across India. Its wider HPV testing programme has analysed more than 20,000 clinical samples, including a five-year multicentric study of 12,373 samples from Maharashtra, Karnataka, Odisha and Itanagar in Arunachal Pradesh. How Prevalent Is HPV In India? The multicentric study found that 2.6% of the samples were positive for high-risk HPV, with approximately 63% of positive cases seen among women aged 31-50 years. HPV16 was the most frequently detected genotype, followed by HPV31, HPV33 and HPV18. The data also showed geographical variation. High-risk HPV positivity was recorded at 2.4% in Maharashtra, 3.6% in Karnataka, 3.1% in Odisha and 6.5% in Itanagar. Another finding was the presence of more than one high-risk HPV type in some women. Among HPV-positive samples, 34.5% showed co-infection with two or more high-risk HPV genotypes. The commonly observed combinations included HPV16 with HPV66, HPV33 with HPV58, and HPV18 with HPV33. Dr. Preeti Helkar, Chief Genomic Scientist, Greenarray Labs, said, “HPV16 and HPV18 are widely recognised as important high-risk types, but they are not the only high-risk HPV genotypes that we encounter. Our data also showed infections involving other high-risk types as well as women carrying more than one genotype. Looking at individual genotypes gives us a more detailed understanding of HPV patterns across different populations and can contribute to surveillance as well as appropriate clinical follow-up.” HPV DNA testing associated with the programme identifies 14 high-risk HPV genotypes: HPV16, 18, 31, 33, 35, 39, 45, 51, 52, 56, 58, 59, 66 and 68. While several HPV tests separately identify HPV16 and HPV18 and report other high-risk types together, individual genotyping can provide additional information on genotype distribution, persistence and co-infections. Nitin Gupte, CEO, Greenarray Labs, said, “For screening to reach more women, it has to become easier to access and simpler to participate in. Self-sampling can help reduce some of the practical barriers associated with conventional sample collection, particularly for women who may otherwise postpone screening. Our focus is on combining easier sample collection with validated laboratory testing and ensuring that screening ultimately leads to appropriate medical follow-up wherever required.” The World Health Organization’s cervical cancer elimination strategy focuses on HPV vaccination, screening and timely treatment. Making screening easier to access, particularly for women who remain outside routine screening programmes, could play an important role in increasing participation and identifying high-risk HPV infections that require further clinical evaluation. 怀孕前接种 HPV 疫苗 可降低早产和主要并发症的风险 - Bioengineer.org

HPV Vaccine Before Pregnancy Tied to Lower Risk of Preterm Birth, Major Complications - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 8 日发表在《英国医学杂志》上的一项大型瑞典研究发现,怀孕前接受四价 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 疫苗 的女性发生主要不良妊娠结局的风险较低,例如早产、极早产、早产前胎膜破裂和生出严重小婴儿。该研究分析了 2006 年至 2023 年间 624,713 名 16 至 35 岁女性首次生育的​​单胎数据,结果显示,这种关联在 17 岁之前接种疫苗的女性中最为强烈。这表明 疫苗接种 的接种时机可能会影响其保护作用。研究结果强调了 HPV 疫苗接种 的潜在益处超出了其在癌症预防方面的既定作用,因为 疫苗 针对四种 HPV 类型,包括 16 型和 18 型,约 70% 的 宫颈癌 病例由这两种类型引起。该研究调整了各种混杂因素,包括吸烟、体重指数和社会经济状况,并发现几种不良结果之间存在统计学上显着的关联。然而,作者指出,这项研究是观察性的,无法确定因果关系。他们还承认需要进一步研究来探索 HPV 疫苗接种 与改善妊娠结局相关的潜在机制。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2006年至2023年期间进行,发布日期为2026年10月8日。
地点与对象
该研究基于瑞典的国家健康记录,涉及 624,713 名年龄在 16 至 35 岁之间的首次生育的​​单身女性。
事件
《英国医学杂志》上发表的一项瑞典大型研究发现,怀孕前接受四价 HPV 疫苗 的女性发生主要不良妊娠结局的风险较低,包括早产、极早产、早产前胎膜破裂和生出过小的婴儿。Bioengineer.org 于 2026 年 10 月 8 日报道了这一情况。
规模、影响与风险
该研究纳入了 92,620 名在怀孕前接种过四价 HPV 疫苗 的女性,约占总人口的 15%。研究结果表明,HPV 疫苗接种 的潜在益处超出了癌症预防的范围,在早产和极早产、自发性早产、早产前胎膜破裂和产下极小的婴儿方面观察到了统计学上显着的关联。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究承认其局限性,包括有关自然流产的信息不完整以及影响结果的未测量因素的可能性。然而,作者认为,该研究的规模、数据质量和设计最大限度地减少了偏差和错误分类,从而能够评估常见和罕见的不良妊娠结局。在进一步的敏感性分析中,结果仍然相似,表明稳健性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV Vaccine Before Pregnancy Tied to Lower Risk of Preterm Birth, Major Complications - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A large Swedish study published in The BMJ on October 8, 2026, found that women who received the quadrivalent human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine before pregnancy had a lower risk of major adverse pregnancy outcomes, such as preterm birth, very preterm birth, preterm prelabor rupture of membranes, and delivering a severely small baby. The study, which analyzed data from 624,713 singleton, first-time births among women aged 16 to 35 years between 2006 and 2023, revealed that the association was strongest among women vaccinated before age 17. This suggests that the timing of vaccination may influence its protective effect. The findings highlight a potential benefit of HPV vaccination beyond its established role in cancer prevention, as the vaccine targets four HPV types, including 16 and 18, which are responsible for about 70% of cervical cancer cases. The study adjusted for various confounding factors, including smoking, body mass index, and socioeconomic status, and found statistically significant associations for several adverse outcomes. However, the authors note that the study is observational and cannot establish cause and effect. They also acknowledge the need for further research to explore the underlying mechanisms linking HPV vaccination to improved pregnancy outcomes.

HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Thursday, October 8, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer HPV Vaccine Before Pregnancy Tied to Lower Risk of Preterm Birth, Major Complications by October 8, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 HPV Vaccine Before Pregnancy Tied to Lower Risk of Preterm Birth, Major Complications Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A large Swedish study published in The BMJ has found that women who received the quadrivalent human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine before becoming pregnant were less likely to experience major adverse pregnancy outcomes, including preterm birth, very preterm birth, preterm prelabor rupture of membranes, and delivering a severely small baby. The association was strongest among women vaccinated at younger ages, particularly before age 17, suggesting that timing of vaccination may play an important role in the magnitude of the protective effect. The findings, drawn from more than six hundred thousand births across nearly two decades of national health records, point to a potential benefit of HPV vaccination that extends well beyond its established role in cancer prevention. HPV is one of the most common sexually transmitted infections worldwide, and most sexually active people will acquire at least one genital HPV infection at some point in their lives. While many infections clear spontaneously without consequence, persistent infection with high-risk types can drive the development of cervical lesions and, ultimately, cervical cancer. The quadrivalent vaccine used in the Swedish study targets four HPV types, including types 16 and 18, which are together responsible for roughly seventy percent of all cervical cancer cases. Since vaccination programmes began rolling out in the mid-2000s, countries with high coverage have documented steep declines in vaccine-type infections, genital warts, and precancerous cervical lesions, providing the epidemiological foundation for the vaccine’s cancer-prevention credentials. What has become increasingly clear in recent years, however, is that HPV infection may matter for pregnancy as well as for cancer. A growing body of evidence indicates that HPV infection itself, and the clinical procedures used to treat cervical lesions caused by the virus, are linked to an increased risk of pregnancy complications. Treatments such as excisional procedures on the cervix can compromise its structural integrity, a recognised contributor to preterm birth and preterm prelabor rupture of membranes. Direct viral effects on placental and fetal tissues have also been proposed as possible mechanisms. Preventing HPV infection in the first place, the reasoning goes, could therefore reduce the downstream cascade of treatments and complications that jeopardise pregnancy. Discover more pregnancy Track Fertility vaccine vaccines Vaccine To test this hypothesis at scale, researchers in Sweden turned to the country’s nationwide health registers, which link individual-level records across vaccination programmes, antenatal care, and birth outcomes. Their study took the form of a population-based case-control analysis nested within a cohort of 624,713 singleton, first-time births among women aged 16 to 35 years in Sweden between 2006 and 2023. Of these women, 92,620, or about fifteen percent, had received the quadrivalent HPV vaccine before pregnancy. Cases were defined as women who experienced at least one adverse pregnancy outcome, spanning preterm birth before 37 weeks, very preterm birth between 28 and 31 weeks, preterm or prelabor rupture of membranes, delivery of a small or severely small baby, stillbirth, or newborn death. Each case was matched with up to ten control women of the same age and year of conception, a design that helps control for calendar-time effects such as changing vaccination coverage and evolving clinical practice. The researchers then adjusted for a wide range of potentially confounding factors, including self-reported cigarette smoking and body mass index at the first antenatal visit, maternal diagnoses and use of assisted reproduction, the mother’s country of birth, and family income and education level. These socioeconomic and behavioural variables are critical in this field, because vaccination uptake and pregnancy outcomes are both patterned by social factors, and failure to account for them can generate spurious associations in either direction. Discover more Check Symptoms Find Treatments Find an OBGYN After these adjustments, quadrivalent HPV vaccination before pregnancy was associated with lower odds of all the adverse pregnancy outcomes included in the analysis compared with no vaccination. Statistically significant associations were observed specifically for preterm and very preterm birth, spontaneous preterm birth, preterm prelabor rupture of membranes, and delivery of a severely small baby. Notably, the risk reduction was generally more pronounced among women who had been vaccinated before the age of seventeen, a pattern consistent with the idea that vaccination is most protective when it prevents infection before first exposure to the virus. The same age gradient has been observed in cancer-prevention studies, where younger vaccination correlates with higher seroconversion rates and greater reductions in cervical lesions. The biological plausibility of these findings rests on several mechanisms. By preventing persistent cervical HPV infection, vaccination may reduce the incidence of high-grade cervical lesions and, with them, the need for excisional treatments that weaken the cervix during pregnancy. There is also emerging interest in whether HPV infection can affect the intrauterine environment more directly, with viral particles or the inflammatory responses they provoke potentially influencing placental function and fetal growth. While the Swedish study did not dissect these clinical pathways in detail, its authors acknowledge that the underlying mechanisms warrant further investigation. What the study establishes is a robust population-level association that is consistent with these mechanistic hypotheses. Discover more Book a Checkup Find Vaccines pregnant Pregnancy Track Pregnancy As with any observational study, no firm conclusions about cause and effect can be drawn, and the researchers are careful to note the limitations. Information on spontaneous abortions was incomplete, and the underlying clinical pathways linking vaccination to better outcomes were not extensively examined. The authors also cannot rule out the possibility that other unmeasured factors may have influenced the results, a persistent challenge in studies comparing vaccinated and unvaccinated populations. Nevertheless, they argue that the study’s scale, its foundation in high-quality national register data, its long follow-up period in a real-world setting, and its case-control design minimised bias and misclassification, enabling assessment of both common and rare adverse pregnancy outcomes. Results remained similar across further sensitivity analyses, suggesting the findings are robust. The researchers place their results in the context of the accumulating safety literature on HPV vaccination in pregnancy. Although vaccination is not routinely recommended during pregnancy, inadvertent exposure has occurred in large numbers of women through vaccination programmes and surveillance studies, and this body of evidence has found no signal of harm to pregnancy outcomes. Against that backdrop, the new findings shift the framing: rather than merely being safe with respect to pregnancy, HPV vaccination may actively confer additional benefits by reducing the risk of adverse events. The authors state that, together with previous research showing the safety of HPV vaccination during pregnancy, their results suggest the vaccine is not only safe but may also protect against adverse pregnancy outcomes. Taken together, the evidence strengthens the case for the global strategy of HPV vaccination and its continued implementation in routine immunisation programmes, the researchers conclude. For public health policymakers, the study adds a pregnancy-outcomes dimension to a benefit calculus already dominated by cancer prevention, potentially strengthening the economic and clinical argument for vaccinating children and adolescents at the youngest recommended ages. For clinicians counselling patients, the message is that timely HPV vaccination may offer protection that reaches into reproductive health years later. As national immunisation schedules continue to expand and newer vaccines extend coverage to additional HPV types, studies of this kind will be essential for quantifying the full spectrum of benefits that population-level HPV control can deliver, from the cervix to the delivery room. B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV vaccine before pregnancy tied to fewer adverse pregnancy outcomes - Contemporary OB/GYN

News Brief
The article published on October 9, 2026, by Contemporary OB/GYN highlights a study suggesting that HPV vaccination before pregnancy may be linked to fewer adverse pregnancy outcomes. The findings, reported by Joshua Fitch, Senior Editor, are based on real-world data and are part of a broader discussion on maternal health and preventive care. While the study indicates a potential benefit, no specific case or death counts are provided, and the risk level is not explicitly stated. The evidence is under review for its implications on perimenopausal care and maternal immunization practices. The article also includes other related topics such as perimenopause, menopause care, and the impact of certain medications on sleep quality, but these are not directly tied to HPV vaccine outcomes. The content is part of a series exploring women's health issues, including the renaming of polycystic ovary syndrome and advancements in ovarian cancer screening.

HPV 疫苗接种 可预防主要妊娠并发症 - Inside Precision Medicine

HPV Vaccination Prevents Major Pregnancy Complications - Inside Precision Medicine
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 7 日发表在《BMJ》上的一项最新研究发现,怀孕前感染 HPV 疫苗接种 与降低主要妊娠并发症的风险相关。这项研究于 2006 年至 2023 年间在瑞典进行,涉及 624,713 名年龄在 16 岁至 35 岁之间、首次生育单胎的女性。研究表明,接种HPV疫苗的女性37周前早产(调整后OR = 0.95)、极早产(OR = 0.85)、自发性早产(OR = 0.95)、早产胎膜早破(OR = 0.93)和严重小于胎龄婴儿(OR = 0.92)的几率较低。研究结果强调了 HPV 疫苗接种 除了癌症预防之外更广泛的健康益处,表明将这些生殖优势纳入未来的政策评估中可以改善 疫苗接种 计划的成本效益平衡。

时间
该研究于 2006 年至 2023 年期间进行,研究结果于 2026 年 10 月 7 日发布。
地点与对象
这项研究涉及瑞典 624,713 名年龄在 16 岁至 35 岁之间的女性,她们在 2006 年至 2023 年间首次生育了一个婴儿。
事件
《英国医学杂志》上发表的一项大型瑞典研究发现,怀孕前感染 HPV 疫苗接种 与降低主要妊娠并发症(包括早产和其他不良后果)的风险相关。
规模、影响与风险
HPV 疫苗接种 与 37 周前早产(调整后 OR = 0.95)、极早产(OR = 0.85)、自发性早产(OR = 0.95)、早产胎膜早破(OR = 0.93)和严重小于胎龄婴儿(OR = 0.92)的几率相关。
应对、状态与不确定性
这项研究的开展和报告中,研究人员指出了 HPV 疫苗接种 的潜在生殖益处,并建议将其纳入未来的政策评估中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV Vaccination Prevents Major Pregnancy Complications - Inside Precision Medicine

News Brief
A recent study published in The BMJ on October 7, 2026, found that HPV vaccination before pregnancy is associated with a decreased risk of major pregnancy complications. The research, conducted in Sweden between 2006 and 2023, involved 624,713 women aged 16 to 35 years who gave birth for the first time to a single baby. The study showed that women vaccinated against HPV had lower odds of preterm birth before 37 weeks (adjusted OR = 0.95), very preterm birth (OR = 0.85), spontaneous preterm birth (OR = 0.95), preterm prelabor rupture of membranes (OR = 0.93), and infants severely small for gestational age (OR = 0.92). The findings highlight the broader health benefits of HPV vaccination beyond cancer prevention, suggesting that including these reproductive advantages in future policy evaluations could improve the cost-benefit balance of vaccination programs.

ADVERTISEMENT Inside Precision Medicine News News & Features asdfasdfasdfasd News & Features Another test to see if data is duped from hubspot News & Features Synthetic Progesterone Enhances Anti-Estrogen Therapy in Early Breast Cancer News & Features Circadian Rhythm Disruption Linked to Higher Dementia Risk Molecular Dx Dried Blood Spot Testing Accurately Measures Alzheimer’s Biomarkers Topics Informatics Molecular Dx Patient Care Oncology Precision Medicine Translational Research Magazine Browse Issues Subscribe Subscribe Get Inside Precision Medicine Magazine Get Inside Precision Medicine eNewsletters Multimedia Webinars Summits Podcasts Videos Inside Precision Medicine Live Learning Labs eBooks Inside Precision Medicine Precision Medicine HPV Vaccination Prevents Major Pregnancy Complications HPV Vaccination Prevents Major Pregnancy Complications October 7, 2026 Share Facebook X Linkedin ReddIt Email Credit: gilaxia / Getty Images Credit: gilaxia / Getty Images Women who are vaccinated against the human papillomavirus (HPV) before pregnancy are at decreased risk of birth complications, research shows. The large Swedish study, in The BMJ, indicates that HPV vaccination has broader implications for women’s and child health beyond its well-recognized benefits in preventing cervical cancer. The benefits of HPV vaccine before pregnancy included a reduction in the risk of pre-term birth, which is a leading cause of neonatal mortality across the globe. The risk reductions were generally greater among women vaccinated among younger ages, noted lead researcher Zhongsong Zhang, a PhD student from the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, and colleagues. “These findings highlight the importance of timely immunization through school-based programs and extend the benefits of human papillomavirus vaccination far beyond cancer prevention to the reduction of adverse pregnancy outcomes,” they reported. HPV is one of the most common sexually transmitted infections and the quadrivalent vaccine targets four types of the virus, including two—HPV types 16 and 18—that are responsible for nearly three quarters of all cervical cancers. Following indications that HPV infection and the treatment of precancerous cervical lesions are linked with adverse birth outcomes, researchers examined whether HPV vaccination might reduce this risk. They studied 624,713 women, aged 16 to 35 years, who gave birth for the first time to a single baby in Sweden between 2006 and 2023. Of these, 92,620—equivalent to 14.8%—had received the quadrivalent HPV vaccination prior to pregnancy. Women who had at least one adverse pregnancy outcome were matched with up to 10 women and, after accounting for potential confounding factors, HPV vaccination was associated with a lower odds of all the adverse pregnancy outcomes studied. These included preterm birth before 37 weeks (adjusted OR [OR] =0.95), very preterm birth between 28 and 31 weeks of pregnancy (OR=0.85), spontaneous preterm birth (OR=0.95), preterm prelabor rupture of membranes (OR=0.93), and an infant severely small for gestational age (OR=0.92). The researchers say the benefits of HPV vaccination may lie directly through prevention infection with the virus and also indirectly through the need for cervical excisional treatment. They believe their findings contribute to a more comprehensive understanding of the broader health implications of HPV vaccination. The team speculated: “Including potential reproductive benefits in future policy evaluations may further shift the overall cost-benefit balance towards vaccination, including in settings where human papillomavirus vaccination has not yet been implemented.” News & FeaturesCancer preventionCervical cancerHuman papillomavirusPregnancy complicationsViral vaccine Also of Interest News & Features | Rise in Infectious Disease-Caused Cancers Spurs Need for More Prevention News & Features | H. pylori May Contribute to Colorectal Cancer Risk Worldwide News & Features | ‘Troubling Increase’ in Women Never Undergoing Cervical Cancer Screening News & Features | Multi-Layered Screening Identifies Potential ADC Targets in Cervical Cancer News & Features | Caspase-1 Inhibition Points to a Possible Lung Cancer Interception Strategy News & Features | Early Biomarkers of Lung Cancer Risk Linked to Air Pollution Podcast Series Behind the Breakthroughs Join host Jonathan D. HPV 如何重塑 3D 基因组 以将 宫颈癌 锁定为耐药状态 - Bioengineer.org

How HPV Reshapes the 3D Genome to Lock Cervical Cancer Into Resistant States - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报日本人文科学大学的 Katsuyoshi Kumagai 及其同事在《医学肿瘤学》上发表的一篇新评论探讨了高风险 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 如何重塑 3D 基因组 以将 宫颈癌 稳定在耐药状态。该研究认为,HPV 介导的 p53 和 RB 通路破坏只是第一步,病毒劫持 宿主 的染色质结构来放大 MYC 和 E2F 等致癌转录程序。这种增强子-启动子相互作用的重新布线可以解释为什么一些 HPV 相关病变进展而另一些则消退。该综述强调了粘连蛋白释放因子(例如 WAPL)和染色质重塑复合物在维持恶性状态中的作用,表明针对这些 宿主 因子可以提供新的治疗途径。它还指出,BRD4 抑制在抑制 HPV16 E6 表达和增强化疗和放疗反应方面显示出前景。研究结果强调了了解病毒和 宿主 基因组 调节在 宫颈癌 治疗 耐药性中相互作用的重要性。

地点与对象
日本,宫颈癌 患者。
事件
日本人文科学大学的 Katsuyoshi Kumagai 及其同事在《医学肿瘤学》上发表的一篇评论讨论了 HPV 如何重塑 3D 基因组 以稳定 宫颈癌 于耐药状态。该综述强调了染色质结构和 宿主 细胞因子在此过程中的作用。
规模、影响与风险
该综述将 HPV 整合到 宿主 基因组 中确定为 宫颈癌 进展的关键因素,并对 治疗 耐药性产生影响。这表明染色质结构和粘连蛋白调节因子(如 WAPL)可能对于维持恶性状态至关重要,而针对这些因素可以提供治疗机会。
应对、状态与不确定性
该综述基于实验研究和临床数据,重点是了解 HPV 驱动的 宫颈癌 耐药机制。它提出了潜在的治疗策略,例如 BRD4 抑制和基于 CRISPR 的筛查,但没有报告确诊病例或结果。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How HPV Reshapes the 3D Genome to Lock Cervical Cancer Into Resistant States - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A new review published in Medical Oncology by Katsuyoshi Kumagai and colleagues at the University of Human Arts and Sciences in Japan explores how high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) reshapes the 3D genome to stabilize cervical cancer in resistant states. The study argues that HPV-mediated disruption of the p53 and RB pathways is only the first step, and that the virus hijacks the host’s chromatin architecture to amplify oncogenic transcriptional programs like MYC and E2F. This rewiring of enhancer-promoter interactions may explain why some HPV-associated lesions progress while others regress. The review highlights the role of cohesin release factors, such as WAPL, and chromatin remodeling complexes in maintaining malignant states, suggesting that targeting these host factors could offer new therapeutic avenues. It also notes that BRD4 inhibition has shown promise in suppressing HPV16 E6 expression and enhancing chemotherapy and radiotherapy responses. The findings emphasize the importance of understanding the interplay between viral and host genome regulation in cervical cancer treatment resistance.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 9, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer How HPV Reshapes the 3D Genome to Lock Cervical Cancer Into Resistant States by October 9, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 How HPV Reshapes the 3D Genome to Lock Cervical Cancer Into Resistant States Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Persistent infection with high-risk human papillomavirus is the principal cause of cervical cancer, yet the familiar story of E6 and E7 disabling the p53 and retinoblastoma protein pathways has never fully explained the clinical behavior of the disease. Why do some HPV-associated lesions persist and progress while others regress? Why do patients with similar viral status respond so differently to chemoradiotherapy, immunotherapy, and targeted agents, and why do relapses occur at all? A new review published in Medical Oncology by Katsuyoshi Kumagai and colleagues at the University of Human Arts and Sciences in Japan argues that a missing piece of the puzzle lies not in the virus itself but in the host cell: the three-dimensional architecture of chromatin, the packaged form of the human genome inside the nucleus. The central premise of the review is that HPV-mediated checkpoint disruption is only the first act. Once the p53 and RB brakes are released, the malignant cell must still stabilize a proliferative, treatment-tolerant state, and the authors propose that this stabilization depends on epigenetic permissiveness and the spatial organization of the genome. Chromatin is not a passive spool of DNA. It is folded into topologically associating domains, or TADs, insulated neighborhoods in which enhancers preferentially contact the promoters they regulate. Structural maintenance of chromosomes complexes, chiefly cohesin, extrude DNA loops that bring distant regulatory elements together, while the insulator protein CTCF anchors domain boundaries. The WAPL cohesin release factor limits how far loops extend, tuning the overall folding landscape. When any of these components are altered, enhancers can be rewired onto inappropriate promoters, a mechanism long implicated in oncogene activation across many cancers. HPV integration into the host genome is where viral biology and chromatin architecture collide. Integration is not a random insertion event; it frequently disrupts host regulatory regions and places viral and host sequences into new cis-regulatory relationships. Recent work cited in the review shows that the cis-regulatory effect of HPV integration is constrained by pre-existing host chromatin architecture, meaning the local folding context determines which genes a viral integration event can actually activate. Long-read sequencing of cervical cancer cell lines has revealed the structural complexity of these integration junctions, and genome-wide analyses have documented allele-specific impacts of viral-human rearrangements on cancer genome regulation. In effect, the virus hijacks the host’s own enhancer-promoter wiring, and the resulting rewiring can amplify MYC and E2F-centered transcriptional programs that drive uncontrolled proliferation. Discover more Learn Engineering Track Biotech Tour Colleges cancer Cancer The review gives particular attention to MYC, the transcription factor whose amplification has been detected even in preinvasive cervical lesions. MYC operates as a transcriptional amplifier, and tumors with rewired enhancer landscapes can become addicted to this sustained transcriptional output, a concept known as transcriptional addiction. That dependency creates a therapeutic opening. Bromodomain-containing protein 4, a BET family protein that recruits transcriptional machinery to active enhancers, has emerged as a druggable node: experimental studies show that BRD4 inhibition suppresses HPV16 E6 expression and enhances chemotherapy response, and separate work demonstrates that BRD4 inhibition sensitizes cervical cancer cells to radiotherapy by attenuating DNA repair. Early-phase clinical trials of MYC-targeting approaches in solid tumors, such as the peptide-based OMO-103, suggest that the long-undruggable MYC axis is finally becoming addressable. Cohesin dynamics add another layer of vulnerability. The authors highlight their own prior finding that WAPL, the cohesin release factor, can induce cervical intraepithelial neoplasia in experimental models even without HPV E6 and E7, when modulated by estrogen signaling. This is a striking result because it implies that perturbing the genome’s folding machinery alone can push cervical epithelium toward premalignant states. Conversely, it suggests that cervical cancers may develop dependencies on specific cohesin regulators to maintain their malignant chromatin configuration. Recent single-molecule imaging of loop extrusion in living cells and studies showing extensive mutual influence among SMC complexes are refining how researchers understand these folding dynamics, and the review argues that such dependencies should be systematically screened in cervical cancer models using CRISPR perturbation and dependency mapping resources such as DepMap. Discover more Health Study Biology Biology Chromatin remodeling complexes themselves are also implicated. Loss of ARID1A, the AT-rich interaction domain 1A subunit of the SWI/SNF complex, has been linked to tumor progression and adverse prognosis in cervical cancer, and emerging clinical data suggest that ARID1A and B2M mutations, together with antigen presentation pathway status, may predict response to definitive chemoradiotherapy. Histone modification enzymes, including the polycomb methyltransferase EZH2 and the demethylase KDM6A, are altered in HPV E6/E7-expressing keratinocytes, indicating that viral oncoproteins reprogram the epigenome from the earliest stages of infection. Three-dimensional chromatin analyses have additionally identified the transcription factor Sp1 as a mediator that programs and reprograms HPV-host epigenetic architecture in cervical cancer, providing a concrete molecular handle on how viral and host regulatory systems become intertwined. Non-genetic plasticity is a recurring theme in the review’s account of therapeutic resistance. Cancer cells can evade drugs by reprogramming their transcriptional states without any new mutation, and rare pre-existing cell variants can seed drug-induced reprogramming within heterogeneous populations. Epigenetic plasticity is increasingly recognized as a hallmark that fuels tumor evolution while simultaneously exposing weaknesses. In cervical cancer specifically, single-nucleus and single-cell RNA sequencing studies have revealed heterogeneous microenvironments and distinct drug responses between squamous cell carcinoma and adenocarcinoma, while spatial transcriptomics is mapping how cancer-associated fibroblasts and immune cells shape the transcriptional landscape. Context-dependent signaling through PI3K-AKT, RAS-ERK, NOTCH1, and the Hippo pathway effectors YAP and TAZ, whose roles vary by HPV type, cooperates with chromatin state to determine whether a cell tolerates treatment or dies. The tumor microenvironment completes the resistance circuit. CXCL8, a chemokine produced within the inflammatory milieu, has been shown to be essential for acquired radioresistance in cervical cancer cells and is proposed as a therapeutic target. HPV-mediated immune evasion mechanisms, the composition of the immune microenvironment, and the cancer-immune set point all influence how patients respond to the checkpoint inhibitor pembrolizumab, which in the phase 3 KEYNOTE-A18 trial improved outcomes when added to chemoradiotherapy for high-risk locally advanced disease. The review argues that chromatin-informed biomarkers, such as enhancer landscapes and chromatin accessibility profiles measured by ATAC-seq, ChIP-seq, and CUT&Tag, should be integrated with microenvironmental data to stratify patients more accurately than viral status alone allows. Methodologically, the authors lay out an experimentally testable framework that leans on public datasets from The Cancer Genome Atlas and the Gene Expression Omnibus, perturbation-guided prioritization using CRISPR activation and interference screens, and computational tools including compositional perturbation autoencoders and in silico perturbation methods that predict cellular responses to genetic and pharmacological interventions. Multimodal artificial intelligence approaches that combine imaging, genomic, and clinical data are highlighted as a route to precision management, and network pharmacology offers a way to target causal resistance mechanisms rather than symptoms. Crucially, the framework depends on experimentally faithful models: authenticated cervical cancer cell lines, patient-derived organoids that recapitulate cervical tissue dynamics and viral oncogenesis, and patient-derived xenograft-style cultures that can predict sensitivity to chemotherapy and radiation. The significance of this review lies in its reframing of HPV-driven cervical cancer as a disease of coupled viral and host genome regulation. Rather than treating E6 and E7 expression as the whole story, it positions chromatin architecture, enhancer rewiring, cohesin dynamics, and epigenetic plasticity as the machinery that converts viral checkpoint disruption into stable, adaptable, treatment-resistant malignancy. If the proposed framework holds up under experimental scrutiny, chromatin-informed stratification could identify which patients need intensified or combination therapy, while vulnerabilities in BET proteins, MYC signaling, cohesin regulators, and microenvironmental cytokines could yield rational combination strategies. For a cancer that remains a major global cause of mortality despite vaccination and screening, shifting the therapeutic lens from the viral oncogenes alone to the three-dimensional regulatory genome they exploit may prove a consequential conceptual advance. Event

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

这些感染导致全球每8例癌症病例中就有1例,研究发现 - 美国今日新闻

These infections are behind 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide, study finds - USA Today
新闻简报A new study published in The Lancet Oncology estimates that about 12% of global cancer cases in 2024 were caused by infections, with Helicobacter pylori and human papillomavirus (HPV) alone accounting for roughly 1.5 million preventable cancers. The study highlights that HPV is the most common viral sexually transmitted infection in the United States, infecting around 14 million people annually. It specifically notes that HPV is most associated with cervical cancer, with approximately 750,000 cases linked to the virus globally. The research underscores the importance of existing vaccines, such as Gardasil, in preventing HPV-related cancers, as cervical precancer rates dropped significantly in vaccinated populations. However, the study acknowledges that cancer reporting is incomplete in lower-income countries, where 77% of infection-related cancer cases occurred, and that the figures are estimates based on available data. The findings emphasize the potential for preventing certain cancers through infection control and vaccination.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The study estimates that about 12% of global cancer cases in 2024 were caused by infections, with Helicobacter pylori and human papillomavirus alone accounting for roughly 1.5 million preventable cancers. The study was published in The Lancet Oncology in 2024, and the findings were reported in October 2026.
地点与对象
该研究的范围是全球性的,重点关注世界范围内的癌症病例。它特别强调了感染对低收入和中等收入国家的影响,77% 的感染相关癌症病例发生在这些国家。受影响的人群包括 HPV 感染者,尤其是美国和其他病毒 患病率 较高地区的人群。
事件
一项发表在《柳叶刀·肿瘤学》上的新研究估计,包括人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)在内的感染导致全球约有1/8的癌症病例。该研究确定HPV是宫颈癌(宫颈癌)及其他HPV相关癌症的主要致病因素,并特别提到HPV 16和18型与这些疾病有关。
规模、影响与风险
The study estimates that roughly 1.5 million cancer cases worldwide in 2024 were caused by infections, with HPV being behind about 750,000 cases (or 4% of new cancer diagnoses). The risk is highlighted as preventable through existing vaccines and treatments, though implementation varies by region.
应对、状态与不确定性
研究作者指出,这些数字是现有的最佳估计值,但世界某些地区癌症报告的差距意味着总数并不准确。该研究利用全球癌症数据和之前的研究来估计与感染相关的癌症的数量,并承认并非所有感染都被追踪。该研究结果基于对与癌症相关的感染的现有知识,该研究强调了 疫苗接种 和预防的重要性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

These infections are behind 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide, study finds - USA Today

News Brief
A new study published in The Lancet Oncology estimates that about 12% of global cancer cases in 2024 were caused by infections, with Helicobacter pylori and human papillomavirus (HPV) alone accounting for roughly 1.5 million preventable cancers. The study highlights that HPV is the most common viral sexually transmitted infection in the United States, infecting around 14 million people annually. It specifically notes that HPV is most associated with cervical cancer, with approximately 750,000 cases linked to the virus globally. The research underscores the importance of existing vaccines, such as Gardasil, in preventing HPV-related cancers, as cervical precancer rates dropped significantly in vaccinated populations. However, the study acknowledges that cancer reporting is incomplete in lower-income countries, where 77% of infection-related cancer cases occurred, and that the figures are estimates based on available data. The findings emphasize the potential for preventing certain cancers through infection control and vaccination.

Please reference surfaced articles to validate AI summary Explore DeeperDive Full Summary A new study published in The Lancet Oncology estimates that about 12% of global cancer cases in 2024 were caused by infections, with Helicobacter pylori and human papillomavirus alone accounting for roughly 1.5 million preventable cancers, highlighting existing vaccines and treatments that could reduce this burden. Millions of cancer cases worldwide, about 1 in 8, have an underlying cause that is not only treatable but preventable altogether, according to a new study. The answer lies in infections, according to research published this month in The Lancet Oncology, including two of the most common infections in the United States. Roughly 12% of new cancer cases reported globally in 2024 could be traced back to just 12 infections, the authors found, meaning roughly 2.3 million cancer illnesses worldwide could potentially have been prevented. Five of those infections accounted for more than 2.2 million of the estimated 2.3 million infection-attributable cancer cases recorded in the study, and two — the common stomach infection H. pylori and the sexually transmitted HPV —were behind about 1.5 million. The good news? According to the researchers, we already know how to stop and treat these infections. The bad news? In some countries, that's not happening. Top five infections associated with cancer, according to study Helicobacter pylori (aka H. pylori): A common stomach infection that can cause stomach pain, bloating, gas and similar symptoms. More than half of the world's population may have an H. pylori infection at some point in their lives, according to Mayo Clinic. It is most associated with gastric cancers, the study found (approximately 760,000 cases, or 4% of new cancer diagnoses worldwide). HPV (human papillomavirus): A common infection with more than 100 types, HPV is the most common viral sexually transmitted infection in the United States, infecting roughly 14 million people a year, according to Cleveland Clinic. Some strains are high-risk factors for certain cancers, including cervical, vaginal and vulvar. It was most associated with cervical cancer in the study (approximately 750,000, or 4%, of new cases). There is a vaccine that is ideally given before a person becomes sexually active. Hepatitis B (HBV): A vaccine-preventable, often asymptomatic liver infection that can range from a short, acute illness to a serious, chronic infection, according to the CDC. Acute infections are more common in people between the age of 40 and 59. It was most associated with liver cancers in the study (approx. 360,000, or 2%, of new cases). Epstein-Barr virus (EBV): A very common viral infection and type of herpesvirus that often does not cause symptoms and most affects children, teens and young adults. Up to 95% of adults experience an EBV infection in their lifetime, according to the Cleveland Clinic. It was most associated with cancers of the head and neck in the study (approx. 260,000, or 1%, of new cases). Hepatitis C (HCV): A viral infection that causes liver inflammation and can lead to serious damage. Like hepatitis B, it can cause a short, acute illness or a chronic, long-term one. It is often asymptomatic; experts estimate that more than 2 million have chronic hepatitis C in the U.S., according to Cleveland Clinic. It was most associated with liver cancers in the study (approx. 160,000, or just under 1%, of new cases). Other infections identified as being linked to cancer included HIV, Kaposi's sarcoma-associated herpesvirus, Schistosoma haematobium (a blood parasite common in Africa and the Middle East), Human T-cell lymphotropic virus type 1, Opisthorchis viverrini (a liver parasite found in Southeast Asia), Clonorchis sinensis (another liver parasite from Southeast Asia) and Merkel cell polyomavirus (a virus known primarily for causing cancer, called an oncovirus). What to keep in mind The study doesn't track every individual cancer case. Instead, it uses global cancer data and previous research to estimate how many cancers are linked to infections. The authors say the figures are the best available estimates, but gaps in cancer reporting in some parts of the world mean the totals aren't exact. Plus, it only counts infections already known to have some association with cancer - there could be others that don't yet have enough evidence to connect the two. Cancer registries are less complete in some regions, particularly in lower-income countries, making it difficult to measure the true global burden of infection-related cancers. This is of particular note for this study, as it found that 77% of infection-related cancer cases occurred in low- and middle-income countries. Eastern Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa and Southeast Asia were among the regions with the largest cancer burden. It's important to remember that while 12% of all global cancer cases is a significant number, most cancers are not caused by infection, at least not according to what scientists know right now. When preventing infection could mean preventing cancer While infection may not be the primary driver of cancer behind all cancers nationwide, it accounts for a disproportionate number of cases within certain types of cancers, such as cervical cancer. The study found that the vast majority of cervical cancer cases can be attributed to HPV, for example. Researchers also noted that this means several cancers could be prevented with tools we already have but aren't being used. In these cases, infection control, which is already a well-established science, could lead to cancer control. While the authors noted that more research is needed, especially for some infection-cancer relationships still being explored, like Epstein-Barr, we already have proven methods of addressing certain risks. Areas with the highest cancer burden do not have as ready access to the resources used to track, prevent and control infection, they found. HPV vaccination is a major prevention tool, the study authors said. CDC data show it can be highly effective in preventing both infections and associated cancers. Within 12 years of the vaccine Gardasil being introduced in the United States, infections of the four HPV types it targets decreased 88% among 14–19-year-old females and 81% among 20–24-year-old females. Cervical precancer rates among screened females dropped 50% in 18–20-year-olds and 36% in 21–24-year-olds in the six years after Gardasil hit the market, and cervical lesions due to HPV types prevented by vaccination dropped by 40% in vaccinated women after vaccines were introduced. Vaccination against Hepatitis B is also available and is recommended by the CDC for all infants, children and adults ages 19-59, as well as adults ages 60-plus at high risk for infection. Vaccination reduces the risk of liver cancer linked to HBV infection. Each adult should be tested at least once in their lives for Hep B, says the CDC, and those at higher risk (people of certain ages, races, family histories and with certain conditions) should be tested regularly. More frequent testing and treatment for H. pylori, which can include stool, breath or scope screenings and antibiotics, can reduce its spread and prevalence and therefore its likelihood of contributing to cancer. Testing and treatment for hepatitis B, hepatitis C and HIV, like H. pylori mentioned above, can not only help reduce transmission, but prevent infections from becoming chronic, which leads to a higher likelihood of cancer. Safer injection practices, condom use, PrEP use and making the tools for these practices more available can help stop the transmission of HIV, hepatitis B and hepatitis C, reducing related cancer loads. Screening and treatment of precancerous lesions, especially in cases of HPV-related cervical and anal cancer, can help with early diagnosis and better cancer-related outcomes. About 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide are tied to these infections — most of them preventable - CNN

About 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide are tied to these infections — most of them preventable - CNN
新闻简报A new study published in The Lancet Oncology found that about 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide may be linked to infections, many of which are preventable. The research, conducted by the International Agency for Research on Cancer, estimates that 2.3 million new cancer cases globally in 2024 were attributable to infections, with human papillomavirus (HPV) and Helicobacter pylori (H. pylori) being the most significant contributors. HPV is predominantly associated with cervical cancer, while H. pylori is linked to gastric cancer. The study emphasizes that prevention measures, such as vaccines and screening, are available for four of the five leading infectious causes of cancer. However, the burden of infection-related cancers is unevenly distributed, with low-income and middle-income countries accounting for 77% of these cases. The researchers stress the importance of public awareness and implementation of existing prevention strategies to reduce the impact of these infections on cancer rates.
查看新闻五要素
时间
本研究的发现基于2024年的数据,文章发表于2026-10-05。
地点与对象
全球范围内,重点关注低收入和中等收入国家,这些国家占所有感染所致癌症病例的 77%。
事件
一项发表在《柳叶刀·肿瘤学》上的研究发现,全球约有1/8的癌症病例可能与感染有关,包括主要与宫颈癌相关的高危型人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)。该研究由国际癌症研究机构的研究人员进行。
规模、影响与风险
The study estimates that approximately 2.3 million new cancer cases globally in 2024 were attributable to infections, with HPV and H. pylori being the two most significant contributors. HPV was linked to cervical cancer, while H. pylori was associated with gastric cancer. Prevention measures, such as vaccines and screening, are available for four of the five leading infectious causes of cancer.
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究基于数据分析,没有提及具体的调查或干预措施。它强调了预防的潜力以及提高公众意识和实施现有措施的必要性。

About 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide are tied to these infections — most of them preventable - CNN

A new study published in The Lancet Oncology found that about 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide may be linked to infections, many of which are preventable. 1 in 8 cancer cases worldwide are tied to these infections — most of them preventable Cancer Vaccines See all topics Facebook Tweet Email Link Threads Link Copied! Human papilloma virus or HPV is one of several infections that scientists connected to about 2.3 million new cancer cases globally in 2024, which is equivalent to 12% of all cancer diagnoses. BSIP/Universal Images Group/Getty Images Millions of new cancer cases around the world may be caused by infections that are either treatable or preventable, suggesting that the cancer could have been avoided altogether. That’s according to new research published in the journal The Lancet Oncology, which found that roughly 1 in 8 cancer cases around the world may be linked to infections – many of which can be prevented through vaccines, screening or relatively straightforward treatments. “We can intervene before it gets anywhere near cancer,” said Gary Clifford, the study’s senior author and an epidemiologist at the International Agency for Research on Cancer. Ad Feedback “We just need the politics and the finances and the buy-in from the public and the communication to get the programs implemented. Prevention works,” Clifford said. “It’s an awareness-raising issue.” Related video CNN A simple blood test can detect 50 plus cancers. Here’s why that’s complicated 4:06 About 2.3 million new cancer cases were attributable to infections globally in 2024, which is equivalent to 12% of all cancer diagnoses, the new study found. And two infections stood out in particular: Helicobacter pylori, commonly known as H. pylori, which was mostly associated with gastric cancers and human papillomavirus, or HPV, which was predominantly associated with cervical cancer. Together, researchers estimate that these two infections accounted for more than 1.5 million cancer cases in 2024. Other infections contributed substantially as well: Hepatitis B or HBV was linked to an estimated 360,000 cancer cases, which were mostly liver cancers. The Epstein-Barr virus or EBV was associated with about 260,000 cancer cases, of which a head and neck cancer starting in the nasopharynx was the main driver. Hepatitis C or HCV was tied to 160,000 cancer cases, mostly liver cancers. Related article This 2015 photo shows a pediatrician preparing a shot of the HPV vaccine for a patient in Cranston, Rhode Island. Keith Bedford/The Boston Globe/Getty Images/File What you need to know about the cancer-fighting HPV vaccine 5 min read “Some infection-related cancers, such as liver and gastric cancer, generally have poor prognosis, meaning that a similar proportion of deaths from these cancers are likely attributable to infection. For other cancers, including cervical cancer and Kaposi sarcoma, infection is necessary for cancer development,” Harriet Rumgay, epidemiologist at the International Agency for Research on Cancer and an author of the study, said in an email. “However, highly effective prevention measures already exist for four of the five leading infectious causes of cancer, as well as for HIV,” Rumgay said, referring to the human immunodeficiency virus. The four infections include HPV and hepatitis B, for which vaccines are available, as well as H. pylori and hepatitis C, for which testing and treatments are available. Clifford, Rumgay and their colleagues analyzed data from the Global Cancer Observatory’s Cancer Today database to estimate the incidence of cancer around the world in 2024. They examined how many of those cancer cases could be attributable to infections and then analyzed each infection-cancer pair in their data set. The new analysis builds on earlier work published in 2018, but the researchers now have a better understanding of the relationships between certain infections and cancer. The new study incorporated 16 additional infection-cancer pairs compared with the previous 2018 global analysis, including some cancers paired with EBV. “EBV is one of these agents for which we knew it was related to some cancers. In fact, EBV was the first ever cancer-causing agent of infection to be discovered in Burkitt lymphoma,” Clifford said. “That was like 60 years ago almost, and that was the first realization that a virus could cause cancer. And since then, we’ve identified it in other cancers, particularly nasopharyngeal carcinomas,” he said. “We were already clear about the link with EBV, even back in that 2018 data. So, it was there, but it wasn’t really fully addressed because we didn’t understand the full extent of the cancer burden that it was causing.” Where the infection-related cancer burden is greatest The new research also reveals that the burden of infection-related cancers isn’t evenly distributed around the world. Low-income and middle-income countries accounted for 77% of all infection-attributable cancer cases, reflecting differences in the prevalence of cancer-causing infections as well as access to vaccines, screening, testing and treatment. But if health officials know which infections are contributing to cancer, and where those infections are most common, they can focus resources on the strategies that have the potential to save lives. Related article Drs Producoes/E+/Getty Images The HPV vaccine can reduce certain cancers by half. Expert explains why men and boys should get it 5 min read “Even within the United States, the burden of infection-related cancer is not evenly distributed, and some populations have higher rates of infections such as hepatitis B, hepatitis C, HPV or H. pylori, while others have less access to vaccination, screening or treatment. And these differences can translate into disparities in cancer incidence many years later,” said Veronika Fedirko, professor of epidemiology at The University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center in Houston, who was not involved in the new study. “Infection-related cancers are certainly an important issue in the United States,” she said, referring to a separate analysis from the American Cancer Society that found in 2019, 3.4% of all cancers among adults 30 and older – which was about 60,310 cases – were attributable to seven infections evaluated: EBV, H. pylori, hepatitis B, hepatitis C, Kaposi sarcoma-associated herpesvirus, HIV and, the largest contributor, HPV. “Having a cancer-causing infection does not mean that a person will develop cancer. These infections increase the risk of certain cancers, but most people who are infected will never develop cancer,” Fedirko said. “Cancer usually develops only in a small proportion of people, often when an infection persists for many years and together with other factors such as age, immune function, smoking, genetics or other exposures.” How to ‘take away’ the risk Cancer is an enormously complicated group of diseases with many different causes and risk factors, including genetics, environmental exposures, aging and lifestyle factors. But the new findings show that infections represent an area where prevention can make a meaningful difference. Prevention efforts can include vaccination and, when appropriate, testing for and treating infections that increase cancer risk. For some cancers, screening can also identify precancerous changes so they can be treated before invasive cancer develops. Related article A new recommendation from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says all adults should be screened at least once in their lifetime for hepatitis B, an illness that's linked to liver disease and cancer. NIAID/CDC All adults should be screened for hepatitis B at least once, CDC says 2 min read Getting tested and following up with a doctor “almost takes away any cancer risk,” Clifford said. Once infections get diagnosed, “we know how to manage them,” he said. “The large majority of these infections will not cause cancer. Some of them will, and we’re learning more about which ones, but that’s a work in progress. The point is: It doesn’t mean that you’ve got a cancer just because you have the infection.” For instance, people can be screened for hepatitis C and treated if they are infected, thus helping to prevent cancer from developing down the road. The same is true for H. pylori. The bacterium is relatively common, and many people who carry it may not immediately realize they have an infection. But H. pylori can cause chronic inflammation in the stomach and become a major risk factor for stomach cancer. Treating the infection with antibiotics can reduce that risk, giving doctors another opportunity to intervene before cancer may develop. Related article Papilloma virus, HPV). It causes cervical cancer. Image taken with transmission electron microscopy. (Photo by: BSIP/Universal Images Group via Getty Images) BSIP/Universal Images Group/Getty Images HPV vaccine reduced cervical cancer rates by 87% in women, UK study finds 3 min read Additionally, HPV and hepatitis B are examples of how vaccination can help prevent cancer. Hepatitis B infection can damage the liver over time and increase the risk of liver cancer, but there is a vaccine available that can help prevent the viral infection. HPV is a group of more than 200 related viruses, which can spread through sexual contact. In some cases, HPV infection can lead to six types of cancer: cervical, anal, penile, vaginal, vulvar and oropharyngeal (mouth and throat). But the vaccine can protect against those cancers since it works by preventing HPV infection. The new research “teaches us the importance of vaccination and how that can prevent different types of cancer,” Dr. Nancy Lee, a radiation oncologist at Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center who was not involved in the new study, said in an email. “This is no different than getting your flu shot to prevent the flu. The public needs to be made aware and vaccination should be reinforced,” Lee said. “Many different types of cancers are caused by infection and if we prevent the infection, the incidence of cancer should be lower.” Other prevention measures that reduce transmission of various cancer-attributable infections include “safe injection practices, condom use, and HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis,” Rumgay said. “If implemented widely and equitably, these strategies could prevent hundreds of thousands, if not millions, of future cancer cases worldwide.” Cancer Vaccines See all topics Facebook Tweet Email Link Threads Link Copied! How HPV Reshapes the 3D Genome to Lock Cervical Cancer Into Resistant States
